partly a matter of overall careful study of the text and its contexts, and
partly a matter of experience. Once again, the hermeneutical circle will be
at work. As you carefully engage the details of a text, an understanding of

--- Page 131 ---
the whole passage will begin to emerge. As it does, you will begin to have a
feel for what elements of the text deserve the most attention and emphasis
in an exegesis paper (or, for that matter, in a homily or sermon). Indeed,
careful attention to details— sometimes the details others have overlooked
—is very often what creates and supports an exegetical thesis. Thus
exegesis of a text, even when using appropriate historical and literary
methods, is more like an art than a science.
 
Some Basic Questions
 
Every genre, indeed every passage, of the Bible is unique. Still, there are
some basic questions about every passage that you need to ask yourself.
Examples of these fundamental questions are:
 • What does the t ext communicate, and how?
• What are the key terms and images? What do they mean?
• Are there any key terms or ideas whose meaning may be explained
by looking elsewhere in the book?
• Are there any literary or rhetorical devices (simile, metaphor,
hyperbole, personification, repetition, irony, etc.), and if so, what is
their effect?
• What kinds of sentences are used? What are the major components of
each sentence? What verbal actions or states appear in these
sentences, and what subjects are associated with them?
• Does the text include appeals to tradition or Scripture, such as stories,
beliefs, laws, and well-known historical figures? If so, how do these
appeals function?
• Does the text appear to use sources, whether written or oral? If so,
how are the sources used?
• If the text is a narrative, what elements of setting, plot (conflict,
suspense, resolution), and character development does each part of
the text, and the text as a whole, convey?

--- Page 132 ---
• Which elements of the text work, individually or together, to instruct,
delight, or move the reader?
• What is the tone, or mood, of the passage, and what elements convey
that tone?
• How do the various parts of the passage reflect and/or address the
situation of the readers?
• How does each part of the passage relate to the other parts?
• How does each part contribute to the whole?
• How does my emerging understanding of the whole affect the
meaning of the parts?
This is a broad range of questions, and more could be added to the list.
Some of these questions are directed primarily at each part (word, sentence,
etc.) of the entire text. Others are directed at the text as a whole. Readers
who ask and answer these kinds of questions will have much to say about a
text.
 
These questions may, in fact, be sufficient for this element of the exegetical
process. The rest of the chapter, however, provides more detailed guidance
on the task of detailed analysis. Specifically, we will look at what the
various parts of a text are and at how the parts relate to one another. Then
we will consider some important aspects of the text as a whole, especially
in light of the sources it may contain or use.
 
The Whole and the Parts
 
A text obviously exists as a whole, as a “relatively self-contained unit of
meaning,” but it also consists of numerous parts. The units of meaning that
an exegete will normally analyze, from the smallest to the largest, are the
following:
 • words—lexical items
• sentence segments—phrases

--- Page 133 ---
• sentences—or utterances
• text segments—paragraphs, stanzas, and smaller clusters of
sentences
• the text itself—as a whole
What these various units of meaning have in common, apart from words, is
that each has a sort of completeness: each is a unit of meaning with a
beginning and end. They differ with respect to size and degree of
completeness and thus with respect to the kind and quantity of meaning
they convey. Students of language generally view the sentence, or utterance,
as the basic unit of meaningful, complete communication, what we
sometimes refer to as a “complete thought.” Sentences usually consist of
phrases, which are made up of words. Sentences work together, so to speak,
to form larger segments of a text, and the text segments combine to form
the text (sometimes called the “discourse”) itself.
 
The process of exegesis works, as we have seen, as a hermeneutical circle, a
back-and-forth movement between one dimension of the text and another—
in this case, between the whole and the parts. One can begin the process of
detailed analysis in several ways—with key words or phrases, for instance,
or with sentences. Eventually, every part of the text needs to be examined.
In the presentation of one’s exegesis in a paper, however, the normal
procedure is to work systematically text segment by text segment,3 within
each segment to work sentence by sentence (which often means verse by
verse), within each sentence to examine each phrase, and within each
phrase to analyze each key word.
 
Since many people are fascinated with the smallest unit of meaning in the
Bible, the word, we will begin our discussion of the parts with it. The length
of your paper will determine the amount of detailed analysis of the text and
its words to include. The shorter the exegetical project, the more crucial it is
to be selective.
 
Key Words and Images

--- Page 134 ---
One of my best teachers of exegetical method used to urge us to treat every
key word or image (word-picture) in the text like an unknown variable—the
x or y—of an algebra equation. For example, in examining the following
verse we should clearly treat the italicized words, and perhaps others, as
such unknown variables:
 
And the Word became flesh and lived among us, and we have seen
his glory, the glory as of a father’s only son, full of grace and truth.
(John 1:14 NRSV)
 
The suggestion to treat key words as unknown variables is good advice, but
some caution is needed in analyzing these words as “lexical items” or
“dictionary entries.” What scholars sometimes call “lexical analysis” and
others “word studies” is an area of exegesis full of potential land mines.
The following discussion suggests why this is true and how to avoid the
dangers of “word studies” while still taking words seriously.
 
Meaning in Context
 
Many people assume that words are the most important conveyors of
meaning. Both modern linguistics and ordinary experience, however, teach
us that meaning is context-dependent. The exclamation “Oh, my God!” has
one meaning in the context of praying and quite another in the context of
admiring someone’s new car. Popularly, people recognize that a word’s
denotation (“literal” meaning) and its connotation (implied or suggested
meaning) can differ, and that a word can mean different things to different
people and in different social locations or contexts. The study of meaning is
called semantics.

--- Page 135 ---
Even apart from the issue of connotation (discussed briefly below), the
meaning of a word is more complex than one might at first imagine.
Linguists suggest that although a word, or lexical item, may have some core
semantic features, it has a spectrum of possible meanings, called its
semantic range.4 The meaning or function of the item depends on a variety
of factors, but especially on the various contexts in which it occurs. These
contexts include especially the speaker’s or writer’s social location, his or
her personal word bank and style (i.e., what this person normally means
when using this term), and the literary and rhetorical setting in which the
term appears. What is the speaker or writer trying to accomplish, and how
does this term serve those purposes? A word has a range of “dictionary” or
commonly recognized meanings (lexical senses), only one of which is its
meaning in context (contextual sense). Furthermore, if a person or group
uses a word for particular purposes and invests its own meaning into the
word, it may take on a new and distinctive meaning for that person or group
(specialized sense). This is sometimes called a technical term.
 
For example, the lexical sense of the word fellowship may be “association
of people with common interests” or “specialized academic appointment
with stipend.” Its contextual sense in the sentence “The church is a
fellowship” is clearly the former, not the latter. However, the general
dictionary sense is further refined in its religious context and may, in some
churches, take on a peculiar, specialized sense, as in “a time of fellowship
after church.”
 
Here the specialized sense is “informal conversation with light
refreshments”—and the conversation may not even be of a religious nature.
Other churches would not use, or perhaps even understand, the term in that
way; it has become a technical term for certain churches.
 
A “biblical” word, therefore, should not be treated as an unchanging,
homogeneous unit of meaning. The distinctive ways in which a word or
phrase is used within a text impart distinctive contextual senses to that
lexical item within that text. For instance, the Psalms use the title son of
God primarily to refer to the king in his special role as God’s representative;

--- Page 136 ---
the Gospel of Mark uses this title to refer to Jesus in a way that invokes
associations with kingship and the power of the Holy Spirit, yet also with
suffering and death; and the Gospel of John associates the title with Jesus in
his eternal closeness to God the Father and, indeed, his divinity. Similarly,
the word salvation has different meanings among the various biblical
writers: political deliverance, forgiveness of sins, and wholeness, to name a
few possibilities. It probably does not mean exactly the same thing for
Isaiah that it does for Matthew, even when Matthew is quoting Isaiah. And
salvation may not mean the same thing for Matthew that it does for another
New Testament writer such as Luke or Paul. It is even possible—and here is
why context is so important—that one writer can use the same word
differently in different contexts.
 
Further Principles for the Analysis of Words
 
A biblical word, then, does not mean the same thing in every place it
occurs. Neither does a word ever mean everything it could possibly mean at
one time. The erroneous notion that a word in a particular context means
the sum total of all (or even some) of its possible meanings is called
illegitimate totality transfer, a term coined by the British biblical scholar
James Barr.5 Illegitimate totality transfer occurs when, for example, a
significant biblical term such as faith or salvation is interpreted in a specific
passage as the conglomeration of all meanings of the word in the Bible, not
according to the usage of the author or according to the context. That kind
of universal synthesis is bad exegesis.6
 
Nor, finally, does a word mean what its origin and history (etymology), or
its component parts, might suggest. Words take on a life of their own (like
texts!) and naturally break away from their roots. For example, the
contemporary usage of the word enthusiasm has nothing to do with “being
in God,” despite its roots in the Greek words for “in” (en) and “God”
(theos). This common misinterpretation of words is called the etymological
fallacy.

--- Page 137 ---
The meaning of a word, then, depends upon a combination of the dictionary
meaning and the context. The importance of these various contextual
principles may be illustrated by consideration of the word(s) green + house.
Let us imagine for a moment that English, like ancient Greek, were written
without breaks between the words, such that the two words green house and
the single word greenhouse would both be written as greenhouse. If we
came upon this lexical item while reading a newspaper column about trends
in exterior home decoration, we could probably assume that green house
(two words) is meant. However, if we saw it while reading a column about
trends in plant care, we could probably assume that greenhouse (one word)
is intended. In neither case, however, would we be correct to transfer the
meaning from one context to the other (i.e., there is no new trend to grow
plants in houses with green exteriors) or to add up the two meanings and
conclude, for example, that people are starting to live in hothouses painted
green. To draw either conclusion would be to engage in illegitimate totality
transfer.
 
Similarly, it would be erroneous to suggest that the words green house, or
even greenhouse, must have something to do with growth simply because
the word green is related to the Old English verb “to grow.” This error is an
example of an etymological fallacy. If a green house or a greenhouse has
anything to do with growth, it is due to factors other than the history of the
word green.7
 
When analyzing the key variables in the literary equation—the words—it is
important to gather as much basic information about these main words as
possible in order then to determine their meaning in context. This
information would include the normal semantic range (possible meanings)
of the word, as well as the usage of the word elsewhere in the particular
book or writer. There are three main types of tools to assist in the
interpretation of words: dictionaries, or lexicons; wordbooks that contain
essays on important biblical words; and concordances, or alphabetically
arranged indexes of biblical words. Of particular importance is the
concordance, which can identify all the places where a word and its

--- Page 138 ---
linguistic relatives (“cognates”) occur. Concordances and other lexical tools
are discussed in section 6 of chapter 11.
 
Connotation and Figurative Language
 
Two additional aspects of word usage that are important for exegesis may
also be mentioned briefly here: connotation and figurative language, or
imagery.
 
We all know that sometimes a word functions outside its normal semantic
range. A word’s “connotation”—the additional meanings the word suggests
within a general cultural context or a particular rhetorical situation, as well
as associated emotions and value judgments—is particularly difficult to
grasp. Knowledge of the historical and social setting of a text is the only
thing that can help. For example, many Americans think that they may
summon a French waiter by calling “Garçon,” but in contemporary France
that term connotes disrespect for the server (even though a French–English
dictionary might include “waiter” as one translation of “garçon”). The
appropriate address nowadays is “Monsieur,” even though most French–
English dictionaries do not provide “waiter” as a translation of “Monsieur.”
 
Figurative language is also a universal phenomenon. Some images are very
culturally conditioned, while others are more universal because they are
based on universal human experiences (such as light and darkness). The
Bible is full of evocative images. These words (and even phrases,
sentences, and longer units) must be interpreted by means of a disciplined
imagination in light of the context in which they appear. Readers should be
particularly attentive to the presence and function of metaphors (e.g., “God
is a rock”) and similes (e.g., “God is like a rock”) in the text. It is not
unusual to find that the key term in a text is in figurative language. Such a
term is sometimes referred to as the governing image or the ruling
metaphor. For example, the first half of Psalm 23 is governed by the image
of the Lord as “my shepherd.”

--- Page 139 ---
Interpreting a Word in Context
 
We have stressed that the meaning of a key word or image in a text depends
on its context(s) and that any kind of “word study” that ignores this
principle will likely misrepresent the word’s significance, at least in part. It
is both possible and important, however, to interpret specific terms in
context—to do word studies in this more nuanced sense—as part of the
exegetical process.8 The following steps may be followed to do so:
 • Consult a good dictionary of biblical words, preferably a lexicon of
the original language, to determine the range of possible meanings
(semantic range) of the word. Note also any interpretation offered
by the dictionary for the specific occurrence(s) of the word in your
text and/or for the general use of that word by that writer or in that
book.
• Select a concordance based on the translation that is the primary
basis for your exegetical work (preferably one that links translated
words to the Greek, Hebrew, or Aramaic terms) or, if you read the
original languages, a Greek or Hebrew/Aramaic concordance.
• Locate the word in question in the concordance, and identify all the
occurrences of that word and closely related words from the same
family (if possible, only those occurrences representing the same
Greek, Hebrew, or Aramaic word or family of words) in the biblical
book or author from which your particular text has been selected.9
• Carefully examine each occurrence of the word/word family in its
context in an attempt to identify its meaning in each context. (There
may be consistency, but there may also be difference.)
• Using the insights gathered from the dictionary/lexicon and from the
study of other occurrences of the word and its relatives, return to the
word in your selected passage. Carefully examine the usage of the
word in this context and in relation to the other occurrences you
have studied, to determine, as best you can, its meaning in the
context of your passage.

--- Page 140 ---
In preparing an exegesis of a text, this process will often need to be
repeated for several key terms in the passage. In the research phase of your
exegesis (see chapter 9), allow your conclusions about the word’s
significance in context to be affirmed, corrected, or expanded by the work
of scholars.
 
The Arrangement of Words into Larger Units
(Phrases, Sentences, and Text Segments)
 
Words do not normally stand alone; they are arranged into larger groupings,
which we may call phrases or sentence segments. The arrangement of
words into phrases and of phrases into sentences is referred to as syntax.
The primary linguistic unit for expressing meaning is not the word or even
the phrase but the sentence.
 
Sentences, in turn, are arranged into larger units of thought and expression
that can be called text segments, which may be as few as two sentences and
as many as a dozen. Such larger text segments are called paragraphs (prose)
or stanzas (poetry). Exegesis requires careful analysis of all of these units.10
 
Phrases are ordered combinations of words that follow patterns of usage
within themselves and in relationship to one another (rules of grammar and
syntax). It is of course impossible to grasp all the nuances of Greek or
Hebrew grammar and syntax in translation, so precise exegesis depends on
in-depth knowledge of the original languages. Nevertheless, exegetes who
do not read the original languages can still pay close attention to questions
of syntax and sentence structure. One can begin the analysis of each verse
or sentence by dividing it into its several segments: the noun phrase, the
verb phrase, and any qualifying or expanding phrases such as prepositional
or adverbial phrases, which perform a kind of “fine tuning” on the language
of the text and are often crucial to the meaning and function of the text.

--- Page 141 ---
As you begin to think systematically about the several verses of your
passage, you will start asking yourself such questions as:
 • What are the main phrases in this text?
• How are these phrases related to one another?
• Is their order significant?
Once the phrases that contain the subject(s) and verb(s) are identified, it is
also important to determine the type of verb(s) (action or state of being) as
well as the tense (time) and mood (e.g., indicative [declarative] or
imperative) of each. What is the significance of these aspects of the verbs?
Are the verb tenses all the same, or are past, present, and future tenses
mixed? If they are mixed, why?
 
Are there verb phrases that relate closely to each other? Are they coordinate
phrases (equal partners), or is one subordinate to another? What word or
other device indicates this relationship? How is the coordination or
subordination important to the meaning of the text?
 
As for the sentences themselves, what kind are they? Each sentence will be
either a declaration, an imperative (either command or counsel), a question,
or an exclamation.11 What is the type of each sentence, and what is the
meaning of that sentence as a unit?
 
As these questions are addressed to the text, and tentative answers come to
mind, it is helpful to create a two-column phrase table. In such a table, you
set out the text phrase by phrase in the left column, skipping several lines
between phrases. In the right column, opposite each phrase, you note your
perception of the meaning and function of each phrase in the text. The
following is an example, for just one verse:
 
Text PhraseMeaning and Function
In the —adverbial phrase indicating time of action and (perhaps)

--- Page 142 ---
beginning the action to follow as the beginning of time
God created—identifies subject and verb; limits creation to God alone;
narrates character of God (creative)
the heavens
and the earth
—two objects/products of creation; encompasses all of
known reality
If you create this table using your computer, you can more easily adjust the
columns and rows to accommodate the additions and revisions that you will
make as you start to think more systematically about the relationships that
exist among the various parts. If you would like to do “sentence
diagramming,” you may wish to try it on part or all of the passage.12
 
The Interrelationships among the Parts
 
We have already considered the fact that exegesis is a “circular” endeavor, a
process of moving back and forth between the parts and the whole (a
“hermeneutical circle”). To understand a text, one must consider not only
the meaning of each of the parts, but also the meaning of the parts to one
another:
 • What are the explicit and implicit relationships of the key words to
one another? of the phrases in each sentence to the other phrases in
that sentence?
• How do the sentences relate to one another?
• How do the sentences work together to become a larger unit of
meaning? Are there any contradictions or discrepancies among the
sentences?
Indeed, we must again stress not only that meaning is dependent on context
but also that meaning is dependent on relationships. Words, phrases,
sentences, and so on do not have meaning in isolation but in relationship
and in context. Some of these relationships are largely explicit, while others
are largely implicit in nature.

--- Page 143 ---
Explicit Relationships
 
There are several typical ways in which the various segments of a text
relate, more or less explicitly, to other segments. These relationships may
exist, for instance, among the phrases of a sentence or among the sentences
within a larger text segment.
 
One obvious set of interrelationships is the one that exists among the
subjects, verbs, and other “participants” in the action of a sentence. For
example, in the opening sentence of the Bible quoted above, one word
indicates the actor (“God”), another word indicates the action (“created”),
and a phrase indicates the resulting product of the action (“the heavens and
the earth”). (These interrelationships are sometimes indicated in biblical
languages by grammatical forms, though the translation of those forms
often requires phrases in English and other languages.)
 
In addition to these interrelationships, there are also phrases and sentences
that expand or qualify other phrases or sentences. These segments of the
text identify, define, describe, or limit other segments. In the example
above, “In the beginning” qualifies the action with respect to time.
 
We may also distinguish between chronological, or narrative, relationships,
on the one hand, and logical relationships on the other. The relationships
are sometimes—though not always—indicated by verbal markers, including
such narrative markers as “before,” “while,” and “after,” and such logical
markers as “because,” “so that,” “therefore,” and “by” or “through.” The
forms of chronological or narrative relationships among parts of a text, and
some of the markers,13 include:
 • simultaneous action—“while,” “when”
• prior action—“before”

--- Page 144 ---
• subsequent action—“then,” “next,” “after this,” “when”
• reaction—“then”
• consequential action—“therefore”
• location of action/setting—e.g., “on Zion”
• time of action—e.g., “on the third day”
The forms of logical relationships among parts, and some common markers,
include the following:
 • condition—“if”
• reason—“because”
• purpose—“so that,” “in order to”
• result or consequence—“therefore,” “so that,” “then”
• means or instrumentality—“by,” “through”
• concession—“though,” “although”
There are also some relationships that can be either chronological or logical
in character. These include comparison (similarity) and contrast
(difference). It should also be noted that certain texts, such as the poetry of
the psalms and certain of Paul’s arguments, have both a logical and a
narrative character to them.
 
Implicit Relationships
 
Not all relationships among the parts of a text are explicit. For example, the
reasons for a character’s action or a writer’s exhortation may not be stated.
Careful analysis may be able to discern important connections that are not
explicitly made in the text; in a paper, the existence of such connections
must not only be asserted but also demonstrated.
 
According to some theories of language, relationships exist in every text
between that which is said and the opposite of that which is said. Every
assertion, in other words, implies the negation of something else. The

--- Page 145 ---
Pauline acclamation “Jesus is Lord,” for example, means that Paul is not
Lord, Caesar is not Lord, and so on.
 
One form of the method known as structural exegesis assumes that every
text contains an inherent pattern, or structure, of opposing convictions.
Daniel Patte, the foremost practitioner of this method, seeks to identify both
the various interrelated beliefs, or “faith convictions,” expressed in a text
and the explicit or implicit “oppositions” to those convictions. Patte and
others look not only for what the author says but also for what the author
means not to say. Thus these exegetes find contrasting subjects, actions,
locations, and so on in every text, all representing a text’s faith convictions
and its explicit or implicit oppositions.
 
Looking for implicit contrasting relationships in a text can generate very
significant insights. Exegetes must be careful, however, in drawing
inferences about opposites, because sometimes two apparently opposite
affirmations can both be true. The Pauline acclamation cited above, for
instance, does not mean for Paul that “YHWH is not Lord”; in fact, quite
the opposite is true. With this caution in mind, however, a good reader will
always look for implicit relationships in a text, whether relationships of
contrast or the kinds of chronological or logical relationships discussed
above.
 
Summary
 
The astute exegete will look carefully at the text to discern and describe its
many interrelationships. Recording observations and questions allows the
kind of “playing with the text” or “thinking on paper” that is necessary for
careful close reading of the text.
 
Dimensions of the Text as a Whole

--- Page 146 ---
It is unlikely that most of the books of the Bible were written without the
use of prior sources, whether oral, written, or both. Quotations, allusions,
and evidence of reliance on other materials abound in the Bible. The
question for exegesis is, What do we do with these phenomena?
 
Sources and Redaction
 
The historical-critical method developed and flourished in large measure
because scholars recognized discrepancies and disjunctures within texts.
This led them to conclude that most biblical texts were like an onion, with
many layers of literary and pre-literary material buried within them, or like
jigsaw puzzles constructed from many discrete pieces, some of which do
not quite seem to fit. The purpose of the historical-critical method was and
is, in large measure, to determine the origin, development, compilation, and
editing of these various sources.14
 
The tasks of the historical-critical method have specific names attached to
them. Source criticism is the task of uncovering the various sources,
especially written sources, that have been used within a text. Form criticism
is the task of identifying small units of tradition (especially oral tradition,
but also written) within a text and classifying them with respect to type or
form (such as hymns, laments, etc.). Form critics also attempt to identify
the kind of situation within Israel or early Christianity in which such a form
would originate and prove useful. Tradition criticism is the discipline that
seeks to identify the ways in which specific forms or oral traditions may
have evolved as they were passed on from place to place and time to time.
Though all of these criticisms focus on the preliterary and/or prefinal form
of the text, they are not particularly interested in the composition of the text
itself. That is the subject of redaction criticism, which is the discipline
devoted to how the final “redactors,” or editors, of the biblical texts adopted
and adapted their available sources for their own purposes. Redaction

--- Page 147 ---
criticism also traces the writer’s interests throughout the biblical book, as
evidenced by trends in the use of the sources and themes. Appendix A lists
some specific examples of the kinds of questions these various criticisms
address.
 
Reactions to the historical-critical method, as defined by these criticisms,
range from staunch defenders and practitioners, to uncompromising critics
who dispute its methodological presuppositions and/or theological value, to
those who see its value but also its limitations and problems. This volume
takes the last of these three positions.
 
No matter how one feels about the methods in principle, in practice they are
difficult to execute, especially for the beginning exegete, but even for the
advanced student or seminary-educated clergy person. Unless an exegete
has detailed knowledge of the original languages, of ancient cultures and
patterns of communication, and of early Israelite and Christian history, he
or she simply cannot carry out these criticisms responsibly.
 
The one possible exception to this problem may be redaction criticism. If an
exegete has good reason to suspect that a writer has used a source,15 and the
source can be identified, then by comparing the text with its source in the
original languages, or even in a good translation, the average exegete can
engage in redaction criticism with some degree of success. For instance, if
the majority of scholars are correct in thinking that Matthew used both
Mark and some other written source, called “Q,” then we may be able to
learn from Matthew’s editing of Mark something of what Matthew wished
to highlight or play down in his Gospel.
 
Redaction criticism asks the following kinds of questions of a text:
 • What evidence of sources is there in this text? Are there differences
in vocabulary, style, and/or literary form? Are there “seams” or
other problematic points in the flow of the narrative or logic? Is

--- Page 148 ---
there an introductory formula indicating a quotation (e.g., “Moses
says . . . “)?
• For what purpose has the writer used the source?
• How has the source been altered? Has it been abbreviated or
expanded? Has it been rearranged? Have words or phrases been
replaced by other words or phrases?
• Do these alterations seem to have an intended effect? Are they
unusual for the writer or document, or are they consistent with other
aspects of the writing in question?
The problems, of course, with redaction criticism are that (1) we cannot
always know for sure that there was a source, (2) we can seldom know for
sure why the source was used and changed, and (3) we cannot assume, if a
writer uses a source, that any differences from the source are either
intentional or significant. Moreover, even if the differences are deemed
significant, we cannot conclude that the differences represent the most
important dimensions of the writer’s literary or theological perspective.
Furthermore, sometimes what redaction critics identify as seams or other
evidence of sources and redaction, literary critics identify as literary or
rhetorical devices.
 
Critics of redaction criticism stress the gravity of these problems and
advocate exegesis of the final form of the text alone, as a literary whole.
While exegesis of the final form of the text is the general approach taken in
this book, it is important to acknowledge both that there are recognizable
sources in the biblical writings and that, with care, the identification of
these sources and the analysis of their use contribute to the exegesis of a
text. In other words, proceed with caution! It would be a mistake to build a
complete exegetical house of cards on a source theory, but it would be
equally grievous to ignore quotations of and allusions to various sources in
the Bible.
 
Intertextuality: Literary and Cultural

--- Page 149 ---
Recent study of the Bible and other literature has emphasized the reality
that much of what people write is a kind of weaving together of the new and
the old. Human literary creativity almost always includes quotations and
allusions to texts, ideas, and images from the culture in which it is nurtured.
That is, texts contain echoes of other texts, as well as reverberations of
additional nonlinguistic items from the cultural environment.
 
This phenomenon is called intertextuality, and it permeates the Bible. As
one scholar says, “the Bible itself is an intertextual fabric within which later
texts elaborate on earlier texts.”16 For example, Isaiah 61 (“The spirit of the
Lord GOD is upon me. . . . He has sent me to bring good news to the
oppressed”), probably written after the Babylonian exile, appears to echo
Isaiah 42, probably written during the exile, and is clearly echoed by Luke
in his account of Jesus’ “inaugural sermon” at Nazareth (Luke 4:16–21).
Interest in intertextuality among biblical scholars is extraordinarily high at
the moment, and will be for the foreseeable future.
 
If we limit the term intertextuality, as some scholars do, to mean the use of
known written texts, then clearly the most important dimension of
intertextuality for the New Testament is its hundreds of quotations of and
allusions to the Scriptures (i.e., the Old Testament). Fortunately, many
editions of the Bible indicate these in cross-references and notes. Even the
books of the Old Testament, or Hebrew Bible, however, seem constantly to
take up and refer to earlier texts or traditions (such as the exodus) that now
are part of the Bible. While this may not always be intertextuality in the
narrow sense of the word, the final product is clearly a woven fabric. Those
attentive to scriptural intertextuality will ask themselves questions such as:
 • Which biblical texts, themes, and/or images does this passage quote,
echo, or allude to? If it cites a text, does it do so verbatim or does it
appear to make changes?
• What aspects of the context of the cited text(s) may have informed
the usage of the text in this passage?
• To what ends does the passage incorporate, modify (if it does so), and
utilize a particular biblical text, theme, or image?

--- Page 150 ---
In addition to the Bible itself, biblical writers sometimes draw on texts
besides Scripture (both religious and secular) and often on extratextual
sociocultural realities. They may quote an ancient proverb or hint at a
famous historical character or event. Or they may allude, for instance, to
royal or imperial images and practices, or to paternal and maternal images
and customs, to portray God.
 
This latter kind of “intertextuality” (drawing on nontextual sources) is
sometimes referred to as cultural intertextuality. The investigation of such
echoes extends the concern for historical context, or “background” (chapter
4), into the detailed analysis of a text.17 Questions similar to those noted on
page 72 of chapter 4 should be raised again during the process of detailed
analysis, but now with more specificity and focus. That is,
 • What are the cultural and political realities, places, customs, values,
and beliefs to which the passage makes explicit or implicit
reference?
• What can be known of the significance of these items at the time in
which the passage was written?
• How does the passage incorporate, modify, challenge, or otherwise
utilize these cultural realities?18
To ignore the Bible’s quotations of and allusions to such texts, ideas,
realities, and images is to neglect one of the most significant aspects of
almost any particular text. The meaning of many texts depends in part—and
sometimes in large measure—on their “interaction” with other texts and
with extratextual cultural realities. For this reason, it is not merely the
historical critic but also the literary critic and the average reader or exegete
who must attend to the “sources”—defined in the broadest sense possible—
that are woven into biblical texts.
 
One problem to avoid, however, is the error of what the Jewish biblical
scholar Samuel Sandmel termed “parallelomania”: the excessive hunt for
parallels as sources for, or as points of contrast to, the biblical text.19
Although there may be many allusions to or apparent quotations from other
sources, this happens in part because a text, idea, or image is “in the air,”

--- Page 151 ---
part of the cultural pool of presuppositions. The wise exegete does not fall
into the trap of thinking that similarity automatically means either
interdependence or deliberate citation. On the other hand, a direct scriptural
quote by a New Testament writer should be understood for what it is and
analyzed for how it has been used by the writer.
 
Narrative Detail
 
A good writer weaves influences and sources together rather seamlessly,
even instinctively. This is especially true of good writers of narrative. Since
so much of the Bible is narrative literature, the analysis of narrative detail
requires some special attention.
 
In some older discussions of exegesis, the term “literary criticism” meant
the analysis of (literary) sources in a text—picking it apart, so to speak.
Today literary criticism can mean many things (from the general study of
the literary aspects of a text to the use of specialized literary theories), but it
does not mean looking for the original forms embedded in the text, its
stages of development, or the purely editorial work of the supposed
redactor. Rather, contemporary literary criticism is interested in the final
form of a narrative text and, more precisely, in how it communicates as a
narrative. The best term for this kind of analysis is narrative criticism.
Narrative criticism is not inherently interested (though neither is it
necessarily disinterested) in the actual community that stands behind the
text and within which the author wrote. Narrative analysis deals with the
story as story and with the author/ narrator and reader/hearer that the text
implies—which may not be synonymous with the actual writer and
audience. (These are often referred to as the “implied author” and the
“implied reader.”) It deals with the final form of the text.
 
As noted in the chapter on form, structure, and movement, narrative
literature follows a pattern of (1) introduction (setting, occasion), (2) rising
action (development, conflict), (3) climax, (4) falling action, and (5)

--- Page 152 ---
resolution (dénouement, closure). Together this sequence of elements forms
the narrative’s plot. The detailed analysis of a narrative text includes careful
attention to which of these elements are present in the text. (These may be
some or all.)
 
It must also include consideration of other narrative elements of the text,
such as character development:
 • Who are the characters?
• How are they developed (through description? their actions?)?
• What are their roles in the story? Is a character a protagonist (leading
character), an antagonist (opponent), or a supporting character?
Attention to invisible characters and forces (such as supernatural powers,
including God) is crucial in the exegesis of many biblical narratives.
 
Astute exegetes will do everything in their power to enter the “narrative
world” of the story. In light of the context of the narrative within the
biblical book as a whole, try to imagine the setting, the characters, the
interrelationships among the characters (including conflict), the mood and
emotions that the story contains:
 • How does the story begin?
• What is the climactic moment of the story?
• How does it end?
After doing this imaginative work, you will be better prepared to engage the
story more critically and creatively. You may wish to consider one or more
of the following in more depth:
 • What aspects of setting are presented in the story and why?
• What elements of the story explicitly or implicitly depict characters
and their interrelationships?
• What is the conflict in the story, and what is the literary and
theological significance of that conflict?

--- Page 153 ---
• What words, phrases, and sentences convey the mood of the story
and its central motif or apparent purpose?
• How do the characters and actions within this narrative relate to other
parts of the larger narrative of which this story or section is a part?
These and similar questions are the questions most of us learned to ask of
literature in school. Some of us have not always been encouraged to ask
them of biblical texts, but they can, indeed must, be asked. Two final points
must be made, however. First, narrative criticism and intertextual analysis
or even redaction criticism are not mutually incompatible. One may inquire
both about how a text adopts and adapts other sources and about how the
text works together as a whole unit and as part of a larger literary entity.
Second, narrative analysis of a text need not be restricted to texts that are
obviously stories. Many texts that are poetic or expository in form contain a
story within or behind them, complete with characters, conflict, and
resolution. Other texts allude to stories or contain parts of large, master
narratives, such as the story of God’s salvation through the exodus or the
cross and resurrection.
 
In some important sense, as many recent scholarly studies of the Bible have
suggested, much of the Bible is narrative literature, and the Bible as a whole
tells a set of interrelated stories. The careful exegete will always be
attentive to the elements of narrative in every biblical text, as well as the
larger narratives to which the biblical texts persistently refer.
 
Conclusion
 
The quantity and character of questions to be considered and addressed in
doing the detailed analysis of a text can be overwhelming. One should not,
however, give up. For one thing, like doing anything else, exegesis requires
practice. As the French say to convey the idea that “practice makes
perfect,” “C’est en forgeant que l’on devient forgeron”: “It is by smithing

--- Page 154 ---
that one becomes a blacksmith.” Or, “It is only by doing exegesis that one
becomes an exegete.”
 
For another, the more you look, the more you will see. The story is told of a
fifth-grade science teacher who once had her class stare at a frog in an
aquarium. The students were supposed to record everything they observed.
When they all thought they had finished, she made them keep staring and
recording. When she told them to put their pencils down, they all replied,
“But we’re not done!”
 
Review & Study
Chapter Summary
 • Exegesis involves balancing attention to the big picture and attention
to the details.
• It is important to select carefully which details to analyze.
• There are some basic questions of detail to ask of every text
concerning key words, images, literary devices, sentence
components, appeals to tradition, sources, narrative and rhetorical
elements, tone and mood, and the relation of the parts to the whole.
• The exegetical process is a hermeneutical circle, moving back and
forth from the parts to the whole.
• Key words and images in a text are like unknown variables in an
equation; their meaning is dependent on context and relationship to
other elements.
• Careful analysis of the phrases in a text, and the function of each
phrase, is an essential part of the exegetical process.
• The parts of the text have both explicit and implicit relationships to
one another.
• Exegesis includes the attempt to identify any sources used by the text
and how they are used.

--- Page 155 ---
• Exegesis must also address intertextuality, both literary and cultural,
or the phenomenon of texts echoing both other texts and extratextual
realities.
• Detailed analysis of narrative texts includes attention to plot,
character development, and the “narrative world” of the text.
Practical Hints
 1. Recording observations and questions allows the kind of “playing
with the text” or “thinking on paper” that is required to read the text
carefully. A two-column phrase table, with the phrases of the text on
the left and space for your observations on the right, may be
especially helpful.
2. Constantly ask yourself, “What do these details mean in the big
picture, and how does the big picture affect the meaning of the
details?”
For Further Insight and Practice
 1. For an idea of the kinds of things you might want to discuss in an
exegesis paper, take a look at what several commentaries say on the
same passage. (Among other things, this exercise will likely reveal
how even scholars disagree with one another on the meaning of a
text!)
2. Read Psalm 1. Compose a brief outline of this psalm and then
analyze the text according to your outline. In your analysis consider
especially the main point of each section, the images and their
respective functions, and the overall meaning of the psalm in light
of these parts.
3. Read carefully Luke 2:1–21 and then divide the story into scenes.
Identify and note briefly the significance of each of the following
aspects of the pericope: date; location (city, “manger”); characters
(human and nonhuman); reactions of the characters to the birth; the

--- Page 156 ---
content of the angels’ message. Answer the following questions:
What is the action in each scene? What is the theme of this story?
What is the tone? Is there any irony in the story? How do the scenes
you identified work together to produce a complete narrative? Does
the story hint at any themes or conflicts that you might expect to
find in the remainder of the book of Luke?
4. Reread Psalm 19. Make a list of about ten to fifteen key words,
phrases, or images whose significance you would need to explore
and explain if you chose to write a paper on this psalm.
5. Read Revelation 21:1–8. Make a list of possible examples of literary
(especially biblical) and cultural intertextuality in this passage, and
note what kinds of questions you might need to answer about these
examples in order to understand the passage.
6. Review chapter 11, section 6, pp. 209–25, “Resources for Detailed
Analysis.”
7. Read carefully the sections of detailed analysis in the sample
exegesis papers in Appendix C.
 1 In addition, there is now something of a trend in commentary writing away from verse-by-verse
analysis and toward the analysis of larger text segments (e.g., paragraphs). This trend, however,
suggests that we can dispense not with the careful consideration of details but only with the notion
that individual sentences (or words) alone bear the most significant semantic weight.
2 It is wiser for the beginning student to move systematically through the text in smaller chunks
than to follow the emerging trend in commentary writing (toward analysis of larger units) mentioned
in the previous note.
3 Given the normal length of exegetical studies, a text segment will usually be a cluster of a few
sentences/verses but may be as short as one sentence or as long as a paragraph.
4 The English word “girl” might be said to have core semantic features that include “human,”
“female,” and (one would think) “young.” Yet the word has a range of possible meanings that can be
found in any comprehensive English dictionary: any female child, daughter, girlfriend, one of a group
of adult female friends, etc. In at least two of these meanings, this word is no longer marked for the
feature “young.” In a specific instance of the word, only one of these dictionary meanings will be the
correct one, and that meaning becomes evident only in relation to its context.
5 James Barr, The Semantics of Biblical Language (Oxford: Oxford University Press, 1961; repr.,
Eugene, Ore.: Wipf & Stock, 2004), 218.
6 One “translation” of the Bible, the Amplified Bible, is based on this thoroughly flawed
understanding of language.
7 An example of this problem in biblical interpretation is the meaning often assigned to the Greek
word translated “church” or “assembly,” 
. Etymologically, the Greek word is derived from the verb “call” 
and the preposition “out” (ek), but its primary significance is probably to be found not in its
etymological roots but in its cultural contexts. The word

--- Page 157 ---
was used of the body of citizens, or members of a guild or other group, assembled to make decisions
for the body; a good translation might be simply “assembly” or “council.” The word was also used in
the Greek Bible to translate the Hebrew word qahal, meaning the assembly of the people of Israel. In
each case the idea of being called or called out might be present, but the primary significance is that
of being assembled.
8 I am grateful to Fr. Michael Byrnes for the suggestion to include this section.
9 For instance, in choosing words to examine, one should normally look at “righteous” as well as
“righteousness” in a particular book (say, Malachi), and “rejoice” as well as “joy” in a particular
book (say, Philippians). In choosing which specific occurrences to examine, the exegete should
remember that a word like “Zion” might have different meanings in Isaiah 1–39 and Isaiah 40–66 if
the second half of the book was not written by Isaiah of Jerusalem in the eighth century B.C.E., as
most scholars think. On the other hand, when studying a passage in Acts, it might be legitimate to
look at occurrences of, say, “peace” in Luke as well, since most scholars believe that Luke is the
author of both books.
10 Some would argue that the paragraph, not the sentence, is the basic unit of meaning. In fact, all
discourse units, from smallest to largest, bear semantic significance. My point here is simply that,
contrary to some people’s thinking, meaning is primarily borne not by words in isolation, or even by
a few key words, but by words in relation to one another in larger units.
11 Declarations, questions, and exclamations generally use verbs in the indicative mood, whereas
imperatives generally employ verbs in the imperative mood (e.g., “He goes,” “Does he go?” and “He
is going!” versus “Go”).
12 Some exegetical handbooks stress sentence diagramming, while others criticize it as a
linguistically flawed approach to sentence analysis. My view is that it can be helpful but that a phrase
chart like the one illustrated in this book is more accessible to most students and can serve equally
well, if not better.
13 The markers noted in the lists are English words. Obviously, the markers will be different
words, or sometimes grammatical forms, in the original languages.
14 For further discussion of the historical-critical method, see the table in Appendix A, certain
resources discussed in section 1 of chapter 11, and John R. Donahue, “Modern and Postmodern
Methods of Critical Biblical Study,” in Scripture (ed. Gorman), 147–62.
15 For example, if it is the scholarly consensus or if the biblical writer quotes a known document.
16 Wim (Wilhelmus) Weren, Windows on Jesus: Methods in Gospel Exegesis (trans. John
Bowden; Harrisburg, Pa.: Trinity, 1999), 200.
17 It has been a commonplace of biblical studies to speak of the “background” of a biblical text,
meaning the general or specific contextual factors about which one needs knowledge to understand
the text. The term “background,” however, is problematic. It erroneously suggests that there is a
distance between the world narrated in the biblical text and the actual ancient Near Eastern or
Mediterranean world, with its beliefs, practices, institutions, etc. Thus I prefer to speak of cultural
intertextuality rather than background.
18 In an exegesis paper, the contextual analysis will briefly consider the aspects of historical,
social, and cultural context that affect the whole passage, while the detailed analysis will treat
extratextual realities that may be associated with only one part of the text (e.g., one verse), and it may
treat in more depth something mentioned only briefly in the contextual analysis.
19 Samuel Sandmel, “Parallelomania,” Journal of Biblical Literature 81 (1962): 1–13.

--- Page 158 ---
5th Element
 
CHAPTER SEVEN
 Synthesis
 
We shall not cease from exploration, And the end of all our exploring Will be to arrive where we started And know the place for the first time. —T. S. Eliot, Four Quartets
 What is the punch line? —J. Christiaan Beker, who asked this of every text and in  every class at Princeton Theological Seminary
 
What is the main point, or what are the main points, of the text?
 
In analyzing the details of the text with the greatest amount of care and
precision possible, you have been dealing with the proverbial trees rather
than the forest. After doing so, however, you must now turn to the forest
and attempt to see what the trees look like as a whole. To return to another
metaphor we have used, it is time to look at the big picture, at the puzzle as
a whole.

--- Page 159 ---
Formulating a Synthesis of the Text
 
To synthesize is to pull various elements together into some kind of unified
whole. Synthesizing, then, is a disciplined but creative act of integration. In
formulating a synthesis of the passage, you are bringing together all that
you have thought or said so far. You are not, however, simply making a
summary; you are drawing a conclusion about the text’s essential meaning,
about its purpose or function, as you have come to understand it. In literary
terms, you are looking to say something about the topic and the theme of
the text—both its subject matter and its slant or perspective on that subject.
Harking back to your original guesswork, you are now turning your
guesswork and careful analysis into a coherent reading of the text as a
whole.
 
As you attempt to synthesize your findings, ask yourself questions like
these:
 • What is the main point of each part of the text, and how do the parts
make a whole? Can the main point be located accurately in one
word, phrase, image, or verse, or does it come only from the content
and/or structure of the text as a whole?
• Why do you think the passage was included in this biblical book?
What might have been the purpose(s) or function(s) of the text for
its original authors and hearers/readers?
• What claims did the text make upon its original hearers/readers?
What response might the author have desired from these
hearers/readers? What effects—intentional or not—might the text
have had on them?
As you ask yourself these questions, never forget that you are reading
religious literature; the authors and their texts are making religious
(theological and spiritual1) claims that are intended not merely to inform
but also to transform their readers.

--- Page 160 ---
The search for a “main point” does not begin at the end of the exegesis or at
the end of the paper. It is part of the process, part of the hermeneutical
circle, as you look at the parts and the whole, the whole and the parts.
Nevertheless, as you approach the conclusion of your work on the text, you
may wish to look back again at the contexts, structure, and details of the
text with the specific purpose of formulating your understanding of the
main point of the text. Some specific questions to ask—which may already
have been asked of the text—include:
 • Is there any repetition of words, ideas, or images that might indicate
not only the structure but also the theme of the text? Is there a
governing idea or metaphor? a central motif?
• Does the structure of the text indicate its focus? Is there a center
point? inclusio? contrast?
• Does the beginning or the end of the text reveal its main concern?
• Are there significant relationships among the various components of
the text that might indicate the overall rhetorical purpose of the text?
After considering the questions suggested above, state your conclusions
clearly and creatively. In the writing of an exegesis paper, there is of course
a close relationship between the synthesis and the introduction, for each
presents your thesis, and the introduction is usually written (or at least
finalized) after the exegetical process is complete. A good, rhetorically
effective synthesis, however, does not only restate the main points you have
made in the detailed analysis or repeat the introduction verbatim. Instead, it
should set out in new (and hopefully memorable) language what the
passage is all about.
 
The art of synthesis should not be undervalued. Although detailed analysis
is very important, one’s synthesis of the passage is an important indication
of the fruits of the exegetical process. As we have said before, exegesis is
not merely the accumulation of unrelated facts and observations; it is,
rather, the production of a reading, an interpretation, of the text. No reader
of the biblical text should be unable to “see the forest for the trees.” For
those who read the Bible as sacred Scripture, this step of synthesis is
extraordinarily crucial, since the task of theological reflection will be
heavily influenced by the reader’s overall understanding of the passage.

--- Page 161 ---
Synthesis and the Plurality of Interpretations
 
Different interpreters are going to arrive at differing interpretations, or
syntheses, of the same text. And the same interpreter, whether exegete or
preacher, will likely have various interpretations of a text depending on the
contexts within which the interpretations take place. Indeed, most of us
hope that a preacher does not say the same thing about a text year in and
year out.
 
In formulating and writing a synthesis—which in effect is the interpreter’s
thesis about the text at a particular point in time— good exegetes present
their interpretations with vigor, but also with humility. They argue against
inferior interpretations when necessary, but they also recognize the value of
other interpretations (and interpreters) and the limitations of their own
knowledge and perspectives. (Admittedly, this last point is not always
embraced by biblical scholars or preachers.)
 
To illustrate both the importance of arriving at a synthesis and the variety of
possible syntheses, we may look at several possible interpretations of a
famous text in Paul’s Letter to the Philippians. Philippians 2:6–11 has been
the subject of great scholarly inquiry and theological reflection for a very
long time. It has often been called a hymn or poem, and it has frequently
been said to predate Paul. The passage famously begins, “Though he
[Christ] was in the form of God, he did not count equality with God as
something to be exploited [or grasped], but emptied himself. . . .” After
marking the voluntary descent of Christ from equality with God to
humanity to a slave’s death—“even death on a cross”—the text says that
“therefore, God highly exalted him” and that all creation will confess that
“Jesus Christ is Lord.”

--- Page 162 ---
Every aspect of this text has been in dispute: the basic meaning of key
words, such as the one translated “exploited” or “grasped”; the theological
significance of startling claims, such as “form of God” and “emptied
himself”; the “background,” or scriptural and cultural echoes present in the
text; the reason Paul quotes or composes the text for a particular historical
and literary context; and the theological implications of the text’s apparent
claims about God and Christ, about their relationship to each other, and
about our relationship to them as readers.
 
As a result, the interpretations of this passage are numerous. A synthesis, or
thesis statement, for the text could legitimately take any of the following
forms (and more):
 • Philippians 2:6–11 is a Christian reinterpretation of the fourth
Servant hymn of Isaiah (52:13–53:12), presenting Christ as the
debased and then exalted Servant of God.
• Philippians 2:6–11 narrates the story of Christ as the preexistent Son
of God whose incarnation and death led to his exaltation by God.
• Philippians 2:6–11 depicts Christ, not as the preexistent Son of God,
but as the true human being in contrast to Adam.
• Philippians 2:6–11 is a Christian reinterpretation of a Gnostic
redeemer myth.
• Philippians 2:6–11 presents Christ in his self-emptying as the
ultimate revelation of true divinity.
• Philippians 2:6–11 presents Christ’s incarnation and death as an
example of love and humility for Christians to imitate (see 1:27–
2:5).
• Philippians 2:6–11 does not present Christ as someone to imitate but
as the sovereign Lord of all who is worthy of Christian devotion.
• Philippians 2:6–11 presents Christ’s death and exaltation as a promise
of resurrection to eternal life for those who suffer as Christians now
(see 3:10–11, 21).
As an exegete, your primary task is to arrive at, argue for, and present one
persuasive interpretation of a text, not to catalog all possible interpretations,
even though you may acknowledge the existence of other views—
sometimes in order to show their deficiencies. Although some (though

--- Page 163 ---
clearly not all) of the interpretations offered above may be mutually
complementary rather than mutually exclusive, they are still distinctive
interpretations. Elements of more than one interpretation may be combined
in an exegesis, but no synthesis can or should include the full range of
possible interpretations, not least because some ways of reading a text are in
fact exclusive of other ways.
 
To be sure, not every biblical text is as rich and open to various perspectives
as is this passage, but many are, and the meaning of a text is never simply
an objective, one-dimensional reality that everyone who reads it will
discover and agree upon. This is true both with respect to historical, literary,
and theological analysis and with respect to the application of the text to
contemporary life, as we will see in the next chapter.
 
At this point, you may be wondering if there is any hope of arriving at a
definitive interpretation of any biblical text. If you are a preacher or future
preacher, you may be asking yourself, “Does all this mean that I cannot
preach with clarity or conviction? If scholars cannot agree on the meaning
of the text, is there any hope for me? Will I ever be able to hear or preach a
word from the Lord?” I believe that there is actually great comfort and both
theological and spiritual vitality in the plurality of biblical interpretations.
But to defend that claim, we need to consider the phenomenon of plurality
in interpretation both linguistically and theologically.
 
Ambiguity and Polyvalence
 
Despite all that we have said about variety in interpretation, well-meaning
students of the Bible sometimes—understandably— hope that careful
exegesis, attention to all the details, will produce a definitive interpretation
of the text: the meaning of this or that favorite, troubling, or otherwise
significant passage. Perusing several commentaries or articles on a
particular Bible passage ought to defuse this understandable but erroneous

--- Page 164 ---
hope. In addition to natural differences in approach or emphasis among
interpreters, there is another factor. Despite—or even because of—careful
investigation of every detail of the text, a good exegete may conclude that
this or that aspect of the text, or even the text as a whole, is ambiguous, or
uncertain, as to its meaning. In some cases, the ambiguity will be due to
insufficient knowledge of the historical and cultural situation in which the
text was produced. In other cases, it will be due to insufficient knowledge
of the vocabulary or grammar used in the text or to unclear arrangements of
words, phrases, or sentences.
 
Ambiguity, however, is not always in the eye of the beholder. It is not
always the “fault” of the reader. Sometimes the text itself is inherently
ambiguous, and sometimes the author intended it to be that way. Deliberate
ambiguity is evocative, allowing or even forcing readers to ponder the text
more carefully, asking “What in the world does this mean?” Ambiguity can
be an invitation to engagement. Readers of John’s Gospel will recall that
Jesus’ ambiguous statement “You must be born anew” or “from above”
(John 3:3, 7) prompted one of that gospel’s most significant exchanges.
 
Ambiguity, whether intentional or not, also allows for various
interpretations of a text, all of which—if arrived at responsibly— have
some claim to validity. In fact, since there is ambiguity and tentativeness in
every historical reconstruction and every literary analysis, and since
exegesis is more like an art than a science, we should expect a text to mean
different things to different people, even if those people employ the same
basic methods of reading the text. A text, in other words, inherently has a
sort of open-ended character that permits a variety of readings. The process
of exegesis brings together a text, a set of contexts in which the text was
created and is now situated, and a reader located in yet another context. The
results of this process are bound to vary. In fact, as suggested above, my
own careful reading of a text this year and next may be quite different. This
is because nearly every biblical text is so richly multidimensional, and its
readers and their contexts so richly varied, that we should speak of the
meanings— plural—of a text rather than the meaning—singular.2

--- Page 165 ---
Specialists in hermeneutics (interpretation) often refer to this phenomenon
as the “surplus of meaning” in texts. Christian (especially Catholic)
theologians speak, particularly with respect to Old Testament texts that have
been understood by Christians to point to Christ, of a text’s sensus plenior.
This Latin phrase means “fuller sense,” or the meaning intended by God but
not necessarily by the human author. For example, since the first century,
Christians have understood Isaiah 52:13–53:12, about the suffering of one
servant of God for the sins of many, as a prophecy about, or at least a text
fulfilled in, the death and exaltation of Christ. This claim does not
necessarily mean that the original author intended a reference to any future
figure at all, much less Jesus of Nazareth. Rather, it means that whatever the
original referent of the Suffering Servant (e.g., a prophet or the people as a
whole), for Christians the fuller meaning intended by God and legitimately
heard by Christians centers on Christ.3
 
For those who hear in and through the Bible the voice of God, this
conviction about the meanings (plural) of a text does not mean that God is
fickle but that God’s voice is not only constant and faithful but also fresh,
new, and creative. The “word of God is living and active” (Hebrews 4:12
NRSV). Nevertheless, a theological account of the surplus of meaning must
include the conviction that there is a significant degree of congruence
between original textual meanings (as far as we can determine them) and
later readings. A suffering servant cannot be morphed into the inspiration
for a military campaign.
 
All this is not to say, then, that a text imposes no limits on our interpretation
of it, or that a text can mean anything we want it to mean. It does mean,
however, that a text has multiple possible readings, both in terms of its
original meaning in context and in terms of its abiding significance, and that
several of these possible interpretations may be legitimate. In the list of
interpretations of Philippians 2:6–11 provided above, there are some that
are different from one another because they look at the text from different
angles, but these interpretations are still compatible with one another. There
are also a few interpretations that are mutually exclusive, such as the two
that give radically different perspectives on the question of Jesus’
preexistence. The task of the exegete is, in part, to discern legitimate from

--- Page 166 ---
illegitimate readings of the text by demonstrating through good historical,
linguistic, literary, and theological analysis that certain interpretations do
not do justice to the historical, linguistic, literary, and theological
phenomena associated with the text. In other words, an interpretation is a
claim, a thesis, and it must be demonstrated. The notion that a text is, or can
be, polyvalent does not excuse sloppy reading or obviate the need for
careful exegetical work.
 
With respect to Philippians 2 and the question of preexistence, the exegete
is faced with numerous phenomena requiring careful analysis in order to
demonstrate the legitimacy of one interpretation or the other. The task of
exegesis will include identifying, interpreting, and weighing the
significance of phrases such as “[being] in the form of God,” “equality with
God,” and “emptied himself” (verses 6–7). These phrases need to be
understood in light of first-century Greek usage, in connection with similar
phrases elsewhere in Paul, and in relationship to the rest of the poem
(Philippians 2:6–11). In my view, these factors all point strongly in the
direction of the text’s affirming Christ’s preexistence, and I would therefore
argue that a denial of Christ’s preexistence in the poem is an illegitimate
reading of the poem. This position, as well as the position it rejects, must be
argued carefully and convincingly (though not here!), not merely claimed.
But if the case for an interpretation is made well, certain other
interpretations will thereby be ruled out even as a variety of compatible
interpretations is allowed.
 
Another textual example may serve to illustrate these two points about the
legitimacy of multiple interpretations and the illegitimacy of unconstrained
interpretation. In this case, we look less at historical and linguistic data and
more explicitly at literary and theological issues. The image of a “city on a
hill” from Matthew 5:14 has a long history of interpretation, much of it
within American religious and especially political contexts. A careful
reading of the text in context is quite provocative. There are echoes of
Isaiah 2, which declares that the nations shall ascend to Jerusalem (Zion)
and live in peace at some future date. This intertextuality suggests Israel’s
—or a renewed Israel’s—eschatological witness to the Gentiles. The image
of city lights on a hill (Matthew 5:15) would be vivid to residents of both

--- Page 167 ---
Judea and Galilee. The nature of this light as “good works” (Matthew 5:16)
gives focus, though not specificity, to the manner of the witness. The
images in the text point to the exemplary mission of a community,
specifically Israel or the early disciples and church as the continuation of
Israel.
 
The Christian church legitimately inherits that image and applies it to itself
in various times and ways. Thus “community” and “light” (= “good
works”), the text’s two dominant images, have been subject to a variety of
legitimate readings. However, the application of these images to a
nonreligious entity (e.g., the United States) for its nonreligious deeds (e.g.,
political freedom, democracy) stretches the limit of interpretation to the
breaking point.
 
Some readings, it has been said, do violence to the text. There must be some
limits to what the text can mean.
 
To use an old analogy, a text is like a diamond with many facets, and no
single observer will see every facet from his or her particular vantage point.
We may refer to this phenomenon as the polyvalence of a text. Polyvalence
is the condition of possessing multiple senses. More precisely, since a text
does impose some restrictions on our interpretation, we should speak of its
limited polyvalence.4 One cannot look at a diamond and legitimately claim
to see an orange, or analyze twelve sentences of prophetic lament and claim
that the prophet is describing the joy of the Lord. But different people can
identify and emphasize different phenomena associated with a text and
therefore offer different, but all legitimate, interpretations.
 
Some people will object to the notion of even limited polyvalence and argue
that what the writer intended to say is what a text meant and means. The job
of the exegete is therefore to discover “authorial intention.” I do believe that
in some cases we can approximate authorial intention and should therefore
state as the synthesis of a text what Paul, for example, seems to have
wanted to say. More often, however, we can only describe what the text

--- Page 168 ---
might have communicated to a first-century c.e. audience in Roman Corinth
or to an eighth-century community in Israel, and also what it communicates
to us with our informed, but still limited, understanding of the ancient
contexts.
 
Discerning authorial intent, as everyday experience teaches us, is always
difficult and sometimes impossible to do. Furthermore, as everyday life
again instructs us, meaning is not equivalent to authorial intent because
communication involves at least two parties, the sender (author) and the
recipient. Although the notion that meaning is created exclusively by the
recipient of a communication is a misguided overreaction to the claim that
meaning is limited to authorial intent, this kind of reaction does rightly
acknowledge the fact that various people receive various messages from a
single communication. Human communication is polyvalent.
 
It is also important to note that many forms of communication are
evocative, rather than merely propositional, and evocative speech or writing
inherently generates several meanings. This is especially true of poetry, and
much of the Bible is poetic literature of one sort or another, from the first
chapter of Genesis, to the prophets, to the psalms; and from the gospels, to
poems such as Philippians 2:6–11, to the book of Revelation. In these cases,
ironically, although authorial intent may not be precisely discernible, it is
very likely that the author intended to evoke a variety of responses simply
by using evocative language.
 
Finally, it is important for those who think about exegesis theologically to
remember that the ultimate author of Scripture is God, and to recall once
again that the word of God is living and active. Divine authorial intent may
be much greater, and yet at times less precisely defined, than the intent of
the human authors. While that conviction does not give readers or preachers
license to engage in eisegesis, it should liberate them to be ready to hear the
voice of God speaking once again through the words of Scripture when they
are read with care and in dependence on the Spirit who inspired those words
and continues to breathe life into them.

--- Page 169 ---
So where does this leave us in the task of synthesis? As each of us attempts
to integrate the various details in the text that we have observed and
analyzed, the different minor conclusions to which various readers have
come begin to add up, and the “grand total”—the understanding of the text
as a whole—begins to emerge. And it may very well be much more than the
sum of the various parts. We can attribute that to human powers of insight,
to the power of the text itself, to the inspiration of the Spirit, or to all of the
above.
 
To conclude: A synthesis creatively expresses one interpreter’s well
conceived, well developed, and well defended conclusion about the
meaning and function of a text. This synthesis will be unique to the
interpreter who offers it. In an exegesis paper, it is the summation of the
argument and the bridge to theological reflection.
 
Review & Study
Chapter Summary
 • Synthesizing is not merely summarizing but rather a creative act of
drawing a conclusion about the meaning and function of a text.
• Several features of a text may provide hints as to its essential topic
and theme.
• For various reasons, including the inherent character of the text itself,
the meaning of a text may be ambiguous.
• Because exegesis is an art (a craft with an inherently subjective
dimension) as well as a science (a technique), and because every
biblical text is multifaceted, a text has a variety of legitimate
readings; it possesses limited, though not infinite, polyvalence.
Practical Hints

--- Page 170 ---
1. Keep a record of the possible key point(s) as you work through the
exegetical process, but save your formulation of the synthesis until
you have time to step back and look at all your details holistically.
2. When you have a sense for the text as a whole, write it down in one
good (even if complex) sentence, trying to use the key terms or
images of the text itself as much as possible while also analyzing the
way the key parts interrelate and constitute the whole.
For Further Insight and Practice
 1. Reread Psalm 19. In one well-written sentence, state your
understanding of the meaning of this psalm, making sure to include
the results of your outline work from your earlier study of it.
2. Read the story about the prophet Elijah, King Ahab, and the
prophets of Baal in 1K ings 18:1–40. What are the chief religious
claims being made in and through this story? What political and
social functions would this text have had when it was first told or
written? Why might the compiler of 1K ings have preserved this
story for future readers?
3. Read the collection of short stories in Luke 8:22–56. How are these
stories related to one another? What seems to be the main point of
each and the point of the collection as a whole?
4. Read carefully Romans 8:28–39 and make a brief outline of the text.
Then state, in one or two sentences, the main point of the text as you
understand it.
5. Review chapter 11, section 7, pp. 225–26, “Resources for
Synthesis.”
6. Carefully read the sections on synthesis in the sample exegesis
papers in the back of this book, Appendix C.
 1 That is, claims about God and about people’s relationship with God.
2 Some interpreters prefer to speak of a text’s singular meaning and its multiple applications.
Even if this understanding of meaning were theoretically correct, it would not functionally alter the
way in which we seek and discover the rich multiplicity of dimensions of meaning that any text
bears.

--- Page 171 ---
3 It is of course also true simply to say, without making any theological claim about divine
intention, that Christians have seen in Jesus the fulfillment of, or ultimate referent in, the Suffering
Servant text.
4 I am less comfortable with the evocative term “restricted infinity”— though I understand its
appeal. See Tate, Biblical Interpretation, 211. Stephen E. Fowl has proposed the term
“underdetermined” reading to describe a variety (though not an unlimited variety) of possible textual
functions, in contrast to “determinate” reading (i.e., determined and restricted, normally by authorial
